</li><li style="line-height: inherit; margin: 0"><a href="/wiki/Adja_language" title="Adja language">Aja</a>

</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Ethnic_group" title="Ethnic group">Ethnic&#160;groups</a> <div class="ib-country-ethnic"></div></th><td <li value="1">Such as <a href="/wiki/Ewe_language" title="Ewe language">Ewe</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Gin_language&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Gin language (page does not exist)">Mina</a> and <a href="/wiki/Aja_language_(Niger%E2%80%93Congo)" class="mw-redirect" title="Aja language (Niger–Congo)">Aja</a>.</li><li value="2">Largest are the <a href="/wiki/Ewe_people" title="Ewe people">Ewe</a>, Mina, <a href="/wiki/T%C3%A9m" class="mw-redirect" title="Tém">Kotokoli Tem</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kabye_people" title="Kabye people">Kabyè</a>.</li><li value="3">Mostly European, Indian &amp; Syrian-Lebanese.</li><li value="4">Estimates for this country explicitly take into account the effects of excess mortality due to AIDS; this can result in lower life expectancy, higher infant mortality and death rates, lower population and growth rates, and changes in the distribution of population by age and sex than would otherwise be expected.</li><li value="5">Rankings based on 2017 figures (<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.cia.gov/the-world-factbook/countries/togo/"><i>CIA World Factbook</i> – "Togo"</a>)</li>
</ol></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Togo</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;t&#39; in &#39;tie&#39;">t</span><span title="/oʊ/: &#39;o&#39; in &#39;code&#39;">oʊ</span><span title="/ɡ/: &#39;g&#39; in &#39;guy&#39;">ɡ</span><span title="/oʊ/: &#39;o&#39; in &#39;code&#39;">oʊ</span></span>/</a></span>&#32;<span class="nowrap" style="font-size:85%">(<span class="unicode haudio"><span class="fn"><span style="white-space:nowrap;margin-right:.25em;"><a href="/wiki/File:En-us-Togo.ogg" title="About this sound"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/11px-Loudspeaker.svg.png" decoding="async" width="11" height="11" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/17px-Loudspeaker.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/22px-Loudspeaker.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="20" data-file-height="20" /></a></span><a href="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/7e/En-us-Togo.ogg" class="internal" title="En-us-Togo.ogg">listen</a></span></span>)</span></span>), officially the <b>Togolese Republic</b> (<a href="/wiki/French_language" title="French language">French</a>: <i lang="fr">République togolaise</i>), is a country in <a href="/wiki/Western_Africa" class="mw-redirect" title="Western Africa">West Africa</a>. It is bordered by <a href="/wiki/Ghana" title="Ghana">Ghana</a> to <a href="/wiki/Ghana%E2%80%93Togo_border" title="Ghana–Togo border">the west</a>, <a href="/wiki/Benin" title="Benin">Benin</a> to <a href="/wiki/Benin%E2%80%93Togo_border" title="Benin–Togo border">the east</a> and <a href="/wiki/Burkina_Faso" title="Burkina Faso">Burkina Faso</a> to <a href="/wiki/Burkina_Faso%E2%80%93Togo_border" title="Burkina Faso–Togo border">the north</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Republic_of_Togo_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Republic_of_Togo-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> It extends south to the <a href="/wiki/Gulf_of_Guinea" title="Gulf of Guinea">Gulf of Guinea</a>, where its <a href="/wiki/Capital_city" title="Capital city">capital</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lom%C3%A9" title="Lomé">Lomé</a>, is located.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> It covers about 57,000 square kilometres (22,000 square miles) with a population of approximately 8 million,<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> and has a width of less than 115&#160;km (71&#160;mi) between Ghana and its eastern neighbor Benin.<sup id="cite_ref-UN_WPP_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UN_WPP-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-UN_WPP_2022_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UN_WPP_2022-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>From the 11th to the 16th century, tribes entered the region from various directions. From the 16th century to the 18th century, the coastal region was a trading center for Europeans to purchase slaves, earning Togo and the surrounding region the name "The <a href="/wiki/Slave_Coast_of_West_Africa" title="Slave Coast of West Africa">Slave Coast</a>". In 1884, <a href="/wiki/German_Empire" title="German Empire">Germany</a> declared a region including a protectorate called <a href="/wiki/Togoland" title="Togoland">Togoland</a>. After <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a>, rule over Togo was transferred to France. Togo gained its independence from France in 1960.<sup id="cite_ref-cia_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cia-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-icye.org_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-icye.org-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> In 1967, <a href="/wiki/Gnassingb%C3%A9_Eyad%C3%A9ma" title="Gnassingbé Eyadéma">Gnassingbé Eyadéma</a> led a successful military coup d'état, after which he became president of an anti-communist, single-party state. In 1993, Eyadéma faced multiparty elections marred by irregularities, and won the presidency three times. At the time of his death, Eyadéma was the "longest-serving leader in modern African history", having been president for 38 years.<sup id="cite_ref-Obituary_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Obituary-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> In 2005, his son <a href="/wiki/Faure_Gnassingb%C3%A9" title="Faure Gnassingbé">Faure Gnassingbé</a> was elected president.
</p><p>Togo is a <a href="/wiki/Tropics" title="Tropics">tropical</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sub-Saharan_Africa" title="Sub-Saharan Africa">sub-Saharan</a> nation<sup id="cite_ref-Republic_of_Togo_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Republic_of_Togo-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> whose economy depends mostly on agriculture.<sup id="cite_ref-icye.org_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-icye.org-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> The official language is French,<sup id="cite_ref-icye.org_13-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-icye.org-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> but other languages are spoken, particularly those of the <a href="/wiki/Gbe_languages" title="Gbe languages">Gbe family</a>. 42.3% of the population adhere to Christianity, making it the largest religion in the country.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Togo is a member of the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a>, <a href="/wiki/African_Union" title="African Union">African Union</a>, <a href="/wiki/Organisation_of_Islamic_Cooperation" title="Organisation of Islamic Cooperation">Organisation of Islamic Cooperation</a>, <a href="/wiki/South_Atlantic_Peace_and_Cooperation_Zone" title="South Atlantic Peace and Cooperation Zone">South Atlantic Peace and Cooperation Zone</a>, <a href="/wiki/Organisation_internationale_de_la_Francophonie" title="Organisation internationale de la Francophonie">Francophonie</a>, <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Economic_Community_of_West_African_States" title="Economic Community of West African States">Economic Community of West African States</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Togo&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: History">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Togo" title="History of Togo">History of Togo</a></div>
<p>Archaeological finds indicate that tribes were able to produce <a href="/wiki/Pottery" title="Pottery">pottery</a> and process <a href="/wiki/Iron" title="Iron">iron</a>. The name Togo is translated from the <a href="/wiki/Ewe_language" title="Ewe language">Ewe language</a> as "behind the river". During the period from the 11th century to the 16th century, tribes entered the region: the <a href="/wiki/Ewe_people" title="Ewe people">Ewé</a> from the west, and the <a href="/wiki/Gen_language" title="Gen language">Mina</a> and <a href="/wiki/Fon_language" title="Fon language">Gun</a> from the east. Most of them settled in coastal areas. The <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_slave_trade" title="Atlantic slave trade">Atlantic slave trade</a> began in the 16th century, and for the next two hundred years the coastal region was a trading centre for Europeans in search of slaves, earning Togo and the surrounding region the name "The <a href="/wiki/Slave_Coast_of_West_Africa" title="Slave Coast of West Africa">Slave Coast</a>".
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Togoland.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/52/Togoland.jpg/220px-Togoland.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="148" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/52/Togoland.jpg/330px-Togoland.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/52/Togoland.jpg/440px-Togoland.jpg 2x" data-file-width="680" data-file-height="458" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Togoland.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Togoland (<a href="/wiki/Rudolf_Hellgrewe" title="Rudolf Hellgrewe">R. Hellgrewe</a>, 1908)</div></div></div>
<p>In 1884, a paper was signed at <a href="/wiki/Togoville" title="Togoville">Togoville</a> with King Mlapa III, whereby Germany claimed a <a href="/wiki/Protectorate" title="Protectorate">protectorate</a> over a stretch of territory along the coast and gradually extended its control inland. Its borders were defined after the capture of hinterland by German forces and signing agreements with France and Britain. In 1905, this became the German colony of <a href="/wiki/Togoland" title="Togoland">Togoland</a>. The local population was forced to work, cultivate cotton, coffee, and cocoa and pay taxes. A railway and the port of <a href="/wiki/Lom%C3%A9" title="Lomé">Lomé</a> were built for export of agricultural products. The Germans introduced techniques of cultivation of <a href="/wiki/Cocoa_bean" title="Cocoa bean">cocoa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Coffee" title="Coffee">coffee</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cotton" title="Cotton">cotton</a> and developed the infrastructure.
</p><p>During the <a href="/wiki/First_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="First World War">First World War</a>, Togoland was invaded by <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom" title="United Kingdom">Britain</a> and France, proclaiming the Anglo-French condominium. The Togoland Campaign involved the successful <a href="/wiki/Third_French_Republic" class="mw-redirect" title="Third French Republic">French</a> and <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British</a> invasion of the German colony of Togoland during the <a href="/wiki/West_African_Campaign_(World_War_I)" class="mw-redirect" title="West African Campaign (World War I)">West African Campaign</a> of the First World War. Following the <a href="/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I" title="Allies of World War I">Allied</a> invasion of the colony in August 1914, German forces were defeated, forcing the colony's surrender on 26 August 1914. On 7 December 1916, the condominium collapsed and Togoland was subsequently partitioned into British and French zones, creating the colonies of <a href="/wiki/British_Togoland" title="British Togoland">British Togoland</a> and <a href="/wiki/French_Togoland" title="French Togoland">French Togoland</a>. On 20 July 1922, Great Britain received the <a href="/wiki/League_of_Nations" title="League of Nations">League of Nations</a> mandate to govern the western part of Togo and France to govern the eastern part. In 1945, the country received the right to send three representatives to the French parliament.
</p><p>After <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a>, these mandates became <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Trust_Territories" class="mw-redirect" title="United Nations Trust Territories">UN Trust Territories</a>. The residents of British Togoland <a href="/wiki/1956_British_Togoland_status_plebiscite" title="1956 British Togoland status plebiscite">voted</a> to join the <a href="/wiki/Gold_Coast_(British_colony)" title="Gold Coast (British colony)">Gold Coast</a> as part of the independent nation of <a href="/wiki/Ghana" title="Ghana">Ghana</a> in 1957. French Togoland became an autonomous republic within the <a href="/wiki/French_Union" title="French Union">French Union</a> in 1959, while France retained the right to control the defense, foreign relations, and finances.
</p><p>The Togolese Republic was proclaimed on 27 April 1960. In the <a href="/wiki/Togolese_general_election,_1961" class="mw-redirect" title="Togolese general election, 1961">first presidential elections</a> in 1961, <a href="/wiki/Sylvanus_Olympio" title="Sylvanus Olympio">Sylvanus Olympio</a> became the first president, gaining 100% of the vote in elections boycotted by the opposition. On 9 April 1961, the Constitution of the Togolese Republic was adopted, according to which the supreme legislative body was the <a href="/wiki/National_Assembly_of_Togo" class="mw-redirect" title="National Assembly of Togo">National Assembly of Togo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> In December 1961, leaders of opposition parties were arrested because they were accused of the preparation of an anti-government conspiracy. A decree was issued on the dissolution of the opposition parties. Olympio tried to reduce dependence on France by establishing cooperation with the United States, United Kingdom, and <a href="/wiki/West_Germany" title="West Germany">West Germany</a>. He rejected the efforts of French soldiers who were demobilized after the <a href="/wiki/Algerian_War" title="Algerian War">Algerian War</a> and tried to get a position in the Togolese army. These factors eventually led to a military coup on 13 January 1963 during which he was <a href="/wiki/Assassinate" class="mw-redirect" title="Assassinate">assassinated</a> by a group of soldiers under the direction of Sergeant <a href="/wiki/Gnassingb%C3%A9_Eyad%C3%A9ma" title="Gnassingbé Eyadéma">Gnassingbé Eyadéma</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ellis93_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ellis93-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> A state of emergency was declared in Togo. The military handed over power to an interim government led by <a href="/wiki/Nicolas_Grunitzky" title="Nicolas Grunitzky">Nicolas Grunitzky</a>. In May 1963, Grunitzky was elected President of the Republic. The new leadership pursued a policy of developing relations with France. His main aim was to dampen the divisions between north and south, promulgate a new constitution, and introduce a multiparty system.
</p><p>On 13 January 1967, Eyadéma Gnassingbé overthrew Grunitzky in a bloodless coup and assumed the presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-heritage.org_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-heritage.org-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> He created the <a href="/wiki/Rally_of_the_Togolese_People" title="Rally of the Togolese People">Rally of the Togolese People Party</a>, banned activities of other political parties and introduced a 1-party system in November 1969. He was reelected in 1979 and 1986. In 1983, the <a href="/wiki/Privatization" title="Privatization">privatization</a> program launched and in 1991 other political parties were allowed. In 1993, EU froze the partnership, describing Eyadema's re-election in 1993, 1998 and 2003, as a seizure of power. In April 2004, in Brussels, talks were held between the <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a> and Togo on the resumption of cooperation.
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Protests_in_Lom%C3%A9,_Togo,_18_octobre_2017_04.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/Protests_in_Lom%C3%A9%2C_Togo%2C_18_octobre_2017_04.jpg/220px-Protests_in_Lom%C3%A9%2C_Togo%2C_18_octobre_2017_04.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/Protests_in_Lom%C3%A9%2C_Togo%2C_18_octobre_2017_04.jpg/330px-Protests_in_Lom%C3%A9%2C_Togo%2C_18_octobre_2017_04.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/Protests_in_Lom%C3%A9%2C_Togo%2C_18_octobre_2017_04.jpg/440px-Protests_in_Lom%C3%A9%2C_Togo%2C_18_octobre_2017_04.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2184" data-file-height="1456" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Protests_in_Lom%C3%A9,_Togo,_18_octobre_2017_04.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <a href="/wiki/2017%E2%80%9318_Togolese_protests" class="mw-redirect" title="2017–18 Togolese protests">2017–18 Togolese protests</a> against the 50-year-rule of the Gnassingbé family</div></div></div>
<p>Eyadéma Gnassingbé died on 5 February 2005. The military's installation of his son, <a href="/wiki/Faure_Gnassingb%C3%A9" title="Faure Gnassingbé">Faure Gnassingbé</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-heritage.org_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-heritage.org-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> as president provoked international condemnation, except from France. Some "democratically elected" African leaders such as <a href="/wiki/Abdoulaye_Wade" title="Abdoulaye Wade">Abdoulaye Wade</a> of <a href="/wiki/Senegal" title="Senegal">Senegal</a> and <a href="/wiki/Olusegun_Obasanjo" title="Olusegun Obasanjo">Olusegun Obasanjo</a> of <a href="/wiki/Nigeria" title="Nigeria">Nigeria</a> supported the move, thereby creating a rift within the <a href="/wiki/African_Union" title="African Union">African Union</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-bbc.co.uk_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bbc.co.uk-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Gnassingbé left power and held elections, which he won two months later. The opposition declared that the election results were fraudulent. The events of 2005 led to questions regarding the government's commitment to <a href="/wiki/Democracy" title="Democracy">democracy</a> that had been made in an attempt to normalize relations with EU which cut off <a href="/wiki/Aid" title="Aid">aid</a> in 1993 due to questions about Togo's human rights situation. Up to 400 people were killed in the violence surrounding the presidential elections, according to UN. Around 40,000 Togolese fled to neighboring countries. Gnassingbé was reelected in 2010 and 2015.
</p><p>In 2017, anti-government protests erupted. UN condemned the resulting crackdown by security forces, and <a href="/wiki/The_Gambia" title="The Gambia">Gambia's</a> foreign minister, <a href="/wiki/Ousainou_Darboe" title="Ousainou Darboe">Ousainou Darboe</a>, had to issue a correction after saying that Gnassingbé should resign.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the February 2020 presidential elections, <a href="/wiki/Faure_Gnassingb%C3%A9" title="Faure Gnassingbé">Faure Gnassingbé</a> won his fourth presidential term in office as the president of Togo.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> According to the official result, he won with a margin of around 72% of the vote share. This enabled him to defeat his closest challenger, the former prime minister <a href="/wiki/Agb%C3%A9yom%C3%A9_Kodjo" title="Agbéyomé Kodjo">Agbeyome Kodjo</a> who had 18%.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> On 4 May 2020, Bitala Madjoulba, the commander of a Togolese military battalion, was found dead in his office. The day of Madjoulba's death came after the re-elected <a href="/wiki/Faure_Gnassingb%C3%A9" title="Faure Gnassingbé">Faure Gnassingbé</a> had his investiture. An investigation has been opened for this case and all individuals around his death are being questioned.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Togo joined the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth</a> in June 2022.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Prior to its admission at the <a href="/wiki/2022_Commonwealth_Heads_of_Government_Meeting" title="2022 Commonwealth Heads of Government Meeting">2022 Commonwealth Heads of Government Meeting</a>, Foreign Minister <a href="/wiki/Robert_Dussey" title="Robert Dussey">Robert Dussey</a> said that he expected Commonwealth membership to provide new export markets, funding for development projects and opportunities for Togolese citizens to learn English and access new educational and cultural resources.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Government">Government</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Togo&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Government">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Politics_of_Togo" title="Politics of Togo">Politics of Togo</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Faure_Gnassingb%C3%A9_2014.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Faure_Gnassingb%C3%A9_2014.png/170px-Faure_Gnassingb%C3%A9_2014.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="229" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Faure_Gnassingb%C3%A9_2014.png/255px-Faure_Gnassingb%C3%A9_2014.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Faure_Gnassingb%C3%A9_2014.png/340px-Faure_Gnassingb%C3%A9_2014.png 2x" data-file-width="457" data-file-height="615" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Faure_Gnassingb%C3%A9_2014.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>President since 2005 <a href="/wiki/Faure_Gnassingb%C3%A9" title="Faure Gnassingbé">Faure Gnassingbé</a></div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Gnassingb%C3%A9_Eyadema,_1972.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Gnassingb%C3%A9_Eyadema%2C_1972.jpg/170px-Gnassingb%C3%A9_Eyadema%2C_1972.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="237" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Gnassingb%C3%A9_Eyadema%2C_1972.jpg/255px-Gnassingb%C3%A9_Eyadema%2C_1972.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/01/Gnassingb%C3%A9_Eyadema%2C_1972.jpg 2x" data-file-width="313" data-file-height="436" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Gnassingb%C3%A9_Eyadema,_1972.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Gnassingb%C3%A9_Eyad%C3%A9ma" title="Gnassingbé Eyadéma">Gnassingbé Eyadéma</a> ruled from 1967 until his death in 2005.</div></div></div>
<p>The president is elected by universal and direct suffrage for five years, and is the commander of the <a href="/wiki/Togolese_Armed_Forces" title="Togolese Armed Forces">armed forces</a> and has the right to initiate legislation and dissolve <a href="/wiki/Parliament" title="Parliament">parliament</a>. Executive power is exercised by the president and the government. The head of government is the Prime Minister who is appointed by the president.
</p><p>President <a href="/wiki/Gnassingb%C3%A9_Eyad%C3%A9ma" title="Gnassingbé Eyadéma">Gnassingbé Eyadéma</a>, who ruled Togo under a one-party system, died of a heart attack on 5 February 2005. Under the Togolese Constitution, the President of the Parliament, <a href="/wiki/Fambar%C3%A9_Ouattara_Natchaba" title="Fambaré Ouattara Natchaba">Fambaré Ouattara Natchaba</a>, should have become president of the country, pending a presidential election to be called within 60 days. Natchaba was out of the country, returning on an Air France plane from Paris.<sup id="cite_ref-BBC_News_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC_News-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> The Togolese army, known as Forces Armées Togolaises (FAT), or <a href="/wiki/Togolese_Armed_Forces" title="Togolese Armed Forces">Togolese Armed Forces</a>, closed the nation's borders, forcing the plane to land in Benin. With an engineered power vacuum, the Parliament voted to remove the constitutional clause that would have required an election within 60 days and declared that Eyadema's son, <a href="/wiki/Faure_Gnassingb%C3%A9" title="Faure Gnassingbé">Faure Gnassingbé</a>, would inherit the presidency and hold office for the rest of his father's term.<sup id="cite_ref-BBC_News_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC_News-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Faure was sworn in on 7 February 2005, with international criticism of the succession.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> The African Union described the takeover as a military <a href="/wiki/Coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="Coup d&#39;état">coup d'état</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> International pressure also came from the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a>. Within Togo, opposition to the takeover culminated in riots in which some hundred died. There were uprisings in cities and towns mainly in the southern part of the country. In the town of <a href="/wiki/An%C3%A9ho" title="Aného">Aného</a> reports of a general civilian uprising followed by a massacre by government troops. In response, Faure Gnassingbé agreed to hold <a href="/wiki/Togo_presidential_election,_2005" class="mw-redirect" title="Togo presidential election, 2005">elections</a> and on 25 February, Gnassingbé resigned as president, and afterward accepted the nomination to run for the office in April.<sup id="cite_ref-Ebow_Godwin_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ebow_Godwin-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 24 April 2005, Gnassingbé was elected president of Togo, receiving over 60% of the vote according to official results. His main rival in the race had been <a href="/wiki/Emmanuel_Bob-Akitani" title="Emmanuel Bob-Akitani">Emmanuel Bob-Akitani</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Union_des_Forces_du_Changement" class="mw-redirect" title="Union des Forces du Changement">Union des Forces du Changement</a> (UFC). Electoral fraud was suspected due to a lack of European Union or other independent oversight.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Parliament designated Deputy President, <a href="/wiki/Bonfoh_Abbass" class="mw-redirect" title="Bonfoh Abbass">Bonfoh Abbass</a>, as interim president until the inauguration.<sup id="cite_ref-Ebow_Godwin_29-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ebow_Godwin-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> On 3 May 2005, <a href="/wiki/Faure_Gnassingb%C3%A9" title="Faure Gnassingbé">Faure Gnassingbé</a> was sworn in as the new president and the European Union suspended aid to Togo in support of the opposition claims, unlike the African Union and the United States which declared the vote "reasonably fair". The Nigerian president and Chair of AU, <a href="/wiki/Olus%E1%BA%B9gun_%E1%BB%8Cbasanj%E1%BB%8D" class="mw-redirect" title="Olusẹgun Ọbasanjọ">Olusẹgun Ọbasanjọ</a>, sought to negotiate between the incumbent government and the opposition to establish a coalition government, and rejected an AU Commission appointment of former <a href="/wiki/Zambia" title="Zambia">Zambian</a> president, <a href="/wiki/Kenneth_Kaunda" title="Kenneth Kaunda">Kenneth Kaunda</a>, as special AU envoy to Togo.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> In June, President Gnassingbé named opposition leader <a href="/wiki/Edem_Kodjo" title="Edem Kodjo">Edem Kodjo</a> as the prime minister.
</p><p>In October 2007, after postponements, elections were held under proportional representation. This allowed the less populated north to seat as many MPs as the more populated south.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> The president-backed party <a href="/wiki/Rally_of_the_Togolese_People" title="Rally of the Togolese People">Rally of the Togolese People</a> (RPT) won a majority with UFC coming second and the other parties claiming inconsequential representation. Vote rigging accusations were leveled at RPT supported by the civil and military security apparatus. With the presence of an EU observer mission, canceled ballots and illegal voting took place, the majority of which in RPT strongholds. On 3 December 2007 <a href="/wiki/Komlan_Mally" title="Komlan Mally">Komlan Mally</a> of RPT was appointed to prime minister succeeding Agboyibor. On 5 September 2008, Mally resigned as prime minister of Togo.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Faure_Gnassingb%C3%A9" title="Faure Gnassingbé">Faure Gnassingbé</a> won re-election in the March 2010 presidential election, taking 61% of the vote against <a href="/wiki/Jean-Pierre_Fabre" title="Jean-Pierre Fabre">Jean-Pierre Fabre</a> from UFC, who had been backed by an opposition coalition called FRAC (Republican Front for Change).<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Electoral observers noted "procedural errors" and technical problems, and the opposition did not recognize the results, claiming irregularities had affected the outcome.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Periodic <a href="/wiki/Protests_against_Faure_Gnassingb%C3%A9" title="Protests against Faure Gnassingbé">protests against Faure Gnassingbé</a> followed the election.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> In May 2010, opposition leader <a href="/wiki/Gilchrist_Olympio" title="Gilchrist Olympio">Gilchrist Olympio</a> announced that he would enter into a power-sharing deal with the government, a coalition arrangement which provides UFC with eight ministerial posts.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> In June 2012, electoral reforms prompted protesters to take to the street in Lomé for days; protesters sought a return to the 1992 constitution that would re-establish presidential term limits.<sup id="cite_ref-BBC20120614_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC20120614-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> July 2012 saw the resignation of the prime minister, Gilbert Houngbo.<sup id="cite_ref-Reuters20120712_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Reuters20120712-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Days later, the commerce minister, Kwesi Ahoomey-Zunu, was named to lead the new government. In the same month, the home of opposition leader Jean Pierre Fabre was raided by security forces, and thousands of protesters again rallied publicly against the government crackdown.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In April 2015, President Faure Gnassingbe was re-elected for a third term.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> In February 2020, Faure Gnassingbé was again re-elected for his fourth presidential term. The opposition had accusations of fraud and irregularities.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Gnassingbé family has ruled Togo since 1967, meaning it is Africa's longest lasting dynasty.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Administrative_divisions">Administrative divisions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Togo&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Administrative divisions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Regions_of_Togo" title="Regions of Togo">Regions of Togo</a> and <a href="/wiki/Prefectures_of_Togo" title="Prefectures of Togo">Prefectures of Togo</a></div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1034237262">.mw-parser-output .stack{box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .stack>div{margin:1px;overflow:hidden}@media all and (min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .stack-clear-left{float:left;clear:left}.mw-parser-output .stack-clear-right{float:right;clear:right}.mw-parser-output .stack-left{float:left}.mw-parser-output .stack-right{float:right}.mw-parser-output .stack-margin-clear-left{float:left;clear:left;margin-right:1em}.mw-parser-output .stack-margin-clear-right{float:right;clear:right;margin-left:1em}.mw-parser-output .stack-margin-left{float:left;margin-right:1em}.mw-parser-output .stack-margin-right{float:right;margin-left:1em}}</style><div class="stack stack-right"><div><div class="floatleft"><div class="noresize" style="height: 369px; width: 150px; "><map name="ImageMap_70866023a335798e"><area href="/wiki/Savanes_Region,_Togo" shape="rect" coords="6,16,73,44" alt="Savanes Region, Togo" title="Savanes Region, Togo" /><area href="/wiki/Plateaux_Region,_Togo" shape="rect" coords="49,232,129,264" alt="Plateaux Region, Togo" title="Plateaux Region, Togo" /><area href="/wiki/Kara_Region" shape="rect" coords="54,100,93,123" alt="Kara Region" title="Kara Region" /><area href="/wiki/Centrale_Region,_Togo" shape="rect" coords="59,164,119,188" alt="Centrale Region, Togo" title="Centrale Region, Togo" /><area href="/wiki/Maritime_Region" shape="rect" coords="67,313,134,340" alt="Maritime Region" title="Maritime Region" /></map><img alt="A clickable map of Togo exhibiting its five regions." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/TG-Regions.png/150px-TG-Regions.png" decoding="async" width="150" height="369" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/61/TG-Regions.png 1.5x" data-file-width="210" data-file-height="517" usemap="#ImageMap_70866023a335798e" /><div style="margin-left: 0px; margin-top: -20px; text-align: left;"><a href="/wiki/File:TG-Regions.png" title="About this image"><img alt="About this image" src="/w/extensions/ImageMap/resources/desc-20.png?15600" style="border: none;" /></a></div></div></div>
</div></div>
<p>Togo is divided into 5 regions which are subdivided in turn into 30 <a href="/wiki/Prefecture" title="Prefecture">prefectures</a>. From north to south the regions are <a href="/wiki/Savanes_Region,_Togo" title="Savanes Region, Togo">Savanes</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kara_Region" title="Kara Region">Kara</a>, <a href="/wiki/Centrale_Region,_Togo" title="Centrale Region, Togo">Centrale</a>, <a href="/wiki/Plateaux_Region,_Togo" title="Plateaux Region, Togo">Plateaux</a> and <a href="/wiki/Maritime_Region" class="mw-redirect" title="Maritime Region">Maritime</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_relations">Foreign relations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Togo&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Foreign relations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Togo" title="Foreign relations of Togo">Foreign relations of Togo</a></div>
<p>While Togo's foreign policy is nonaligned, it has historical and cultural ties with western Europe, especially France and Germany. Togo recognizes the People's Republic of China, <a href="/wiki/North_Korea" title="North Korea">North Korea</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Cuba" title="Cuba">Cuba</a>. It re-established relations with Israel in 1987. Togo pursues an active foreign policy and participates in international organizations. It is particularly active in <a href="/wiki/West_Africa" title="West Africa">West African</a> regional affairs and in the <a href="/wiki/African_Union" title="African Union">African Union</a>.
</p><p>In 2017, Togo signed the UN <a href="/wiki/Treaty_on_the_Prohibition_of_Nuclear_Weapons" title="Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons">treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Togo joined the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth of Nations</a>, along with <a href="/wiki/Gabon" title="Gabon">Gabon</a>, at the <a href="/wiki/2022_Commonwealth_Heads_of_Government_Meeting" title="2022 Commonwealth Heads of Government Meeting">2022 Commonwealth Heads of Government Meeting</a> in <a href="/wiki/Kigali" title="Kigali">Kigali</a>, Rwanda.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> In joining the Commonwealth, Foreign Minister <a href="/wiki/Robert_Dussey" title="Robert Dussey">Robert Dussey</a> told <a href="/wiki/Reuters" title="Reuters">Reuters</a><i>,</i> the country sought to expand its "diplomatic, political and economic network" and to "forge closer ties with the <a href="/wiki/English-speaking_world" title="English-speaking world">anglophone world</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-:2_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Military">Military</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Togo&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Military">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Togolese_Armed_Forces" title="Togolese Armed Forces">Togolese Armed Forces</a></div>
<p>FAT (<span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">Forces armées togolaises</i></span>, "Togolese armed forces"), consists of the army, navy, air force, and <a href="/wiki/Gendarmerie" title="Gendarmerie">gendarmerie</a>. Total military expenditures during the <a href="/wiki/Fiscal_year" title="Fiscal year">fiscal year</a> of 2005 totalled 1.6% of the country's <a href="/wiki/GDP" class="mw-redirect" title="GDP">GDP</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-cia_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cia-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Military bases exist in <a href="/wiki/Lom%C3%A9" title="Lomé">Lomé</a>, Temedja, <a href="/wiki/Kara,_Togo" title="Kara, Togo">Kara</a>, <a href="/wiki/Niamtougou" title="Niamtougou">Niamtougou</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Dapaong" title="Dapaong">Dapaong</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-fat_organisation_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fat_organisation-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> The current Chief of the <a href="/wiki/Staff_(military)" title="Staff (military)">General Staff</a> is Brigadier General Titikpina Atcha Mohamed, who took office on 19 May 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-fat_tetedefat_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fat_tetedefat-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> The air force is equipped with <a href="/wiki/Dassault/Dornier_Alpha_Jet" title="Dassault/Dornier Alpha Jet">Alpha jets</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Human_rights">Human rights</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Togo&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Human rights">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Human_rights_in_Togo" title="Human rights in Togo">Human rights in Togo</a></div>
<p>Togo was labeled "Not Free" by <a href="/wiki/Freedom_House" title="Freedom House">Freedom House</a> from 1972 to 1998 and from 2002 to 2006, and has been categorized as "Partly Free" from 1999 to 2001 and from 2007. According to a <a href="/wiki/United_States_Department_of_State" title="United States Department of State">U.S. State Department</a> report based on conditions in 2010, human rights problems include "security force use of excessive force, including <a href="/wiki/Torture" title="Torture">torture</a>, which resulted in deaths and injuries; official impunity; harsh and life-threatening prison conditions; arbitrary arrests and detention; lengthy pretrial detention; executive influence over the judiciary; infringement of citizens' privacy rights; restrictions on <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_the_press" title="Freedom of the press">freedoms of press</a>, <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_Assembly" class="mw-redirect" title="Freedom of Assembly">assembly</a>, and movement; official corruption; discrimination and violence against women; child abuse, including <a href="/wiki/Female_genital_mutilation" title="Female genital mutilation">female genital mutilation</a> (FGM), and sexual exploitation of children; regional and ethnic discrimination; trafficking in persons, especially women and children; societal discrimination against persons with disabilities; official and societal discrimination against homosexual persons; societal discrimination against persons with <a href="/wiki/Human_immunodeficiency_virus" class="mw-redirect" title="Human immunodeficiency virus">HIV</a>; and forced labor, including by children."<sup id="cite_ref-2010_Human_Rights_Report:_Togo_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2010_Human_Rights_Report:_Togo-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Same-sex sexual activity is illegal in Togo,<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> with a penalty of one to three years imprisonment.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geography">Geography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Togo&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Geography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Togo" title="Geography of Togo">Geography of Togo</a></div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti">.mw-parser-output .tmulti .multiimageinner{display:flex;flex-direction:column}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{display:flex;flex-direction:row;clear:left;flex-wrap:wrap;width:100%;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{margin:1px;float:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .theader{clear:both;font-weight:bold;text-align:center;align-self:center;background-color:transparent;width:100%}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbcaption{background-color:transparent}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-left{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-right{text-align:right}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-center{text-align:center}@media all and (max-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbinner{width:100%!important;box-sizing:border-box;max-width:none!important;align-items:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{justify-content:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{float:none!important;max-width:100%!important;box-sizing:border-box;text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle .thumbcaption{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow>.thumbcaption{text-align:center}}</style><div class="thumb tmulti tright"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:322px;max-width:322px"><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:153px;max-width:153px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:176px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:Ghana_Topography.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Ghana_Topography.png/151px-Ghana_Topography.png" decoding="async" width="151" height="177" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Ghana_Topography.png/227px-Ghana_Topography.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Ghana_Topography.png/302px-Ghana_Topography.png 2x" data-file-width="794" data-file-height="931" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption">Ghana-Togo <a href="/wiki/Topography" title="Topography">topography</a>−<a href="/wiki/Topographic_map" title="Topographic map">topographic map</a></div></div><div class="tsingle" style="width:165px;max-width:165px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:176px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:To-map.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/To-map.png/163px-To-map.png" decoding="async" width="163" height="176" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/To-map.png/245px-To-map.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/5/5e/To-map.png 2x" data-file-width="324" data-file-height="350" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption">Map of Togo</div></div></div></div></div>
<p>It has an area equal to 56,785&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (21,925&#160;sq&#160;mi). It borders the <a href="/wiki/Bight_of_Benin" title="Bight of Benin">Bight of Benin</a> in the south; <a href="/wiki/Ghana" title="Ghana">Ghana</a> lies to the west; <a href="/wiki/Benin" title="Benin">Benin</a> to the east; and to the north, it is bound by <a href="/wiki/Burkina_Faso" title="Burkina Faso">Burkina Faso</a>. It lies mostly between latitudes <a href="/wiki/6th_parallel_north" title="6th parallel north">6°</a> and <a href="/wiki/11th_parallel_north" title="11th parallel north">11°N</a>, and longitudes <a href="/wiki/Prime_meridian" title="Prime meridian">0°</a> and <a href="/wiki/2nd_meridian_east" title="2nd meridian east">2°E</a>.
</p><p>The coast of Togo in the <a href="/wiki/Gulf_of_Guinea" title="Gulf of Guinea">Gulf of Guinea</a> is 56&#160;km (35 miles) long and consists of lagoons with sandy beaches. In the north, the land is characterized by a rolling <a href="/wiki/Savanna" title="Savanna">savanna</a> in contrast to the center of the country, which is characterized by hills. The south of Togo is characterized by a <a href="/wiki/Guinean_forest-savanna_mosaic" class="mw-redirect" title="Guinean forest-savanna mosaic">savanna and woodland plateau</a> which reaches a coastal plain with lagoons and marshes. The highest mountain of the country is the <a href="/wiki/Mont_Agou" class="mw-redirect" title="Mont Agou">Mont Agou</a> at 986 metres (3235') above sea level. The longest <a href="/wiki/River" title="River">river</a> is the <a href="/wiki/Mono_River" title="Mono River">Mono River</a> with a length of 400&#160;km (250 miles). It runs from north to south.
</p><p>The climate is "generally tropical"<sup id="cite_ref-icye.org_13-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-icye.org-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> with average temperatures ranging from 23&#160;°C (73&#160;°F) on the coast to about 30&#160;°C (86&#160;°F) in the northernmost regions, with a drier climate and characteristics of a <a href="/wiki/Tropical_and_subtropical_grasslands,_savannas,_and_shrublands" title="Tropical and subtropical grasslands, savannas, and shrublands">tropical savanna</a>.
</p><p>Togo contains three terrestrial ecoregions: <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Guinean_forests" title="Eastern Guinean forests">Eastern Guinean forests</a>, <a href="/wiki/Guinean_forest-savanna_mosaic" class="mw-redirect" title="Guinean forest-savanna mosaic">Guinean forest-savanna mosaic</a>, and <a href="/wiki/West_Sudanian_savanna" title="West Sudanian savanna">West Sudanian savanna</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-DinersteinOlson2017_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DinersteinOlson2017-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> The coast of Togo is characterized by <a href="/wiki/Marshes" class="mw-redirect" title="Marshes">marshes</a> and <a href="/wiki/Mangroves" class="mw-redirect" title="Mangroves">mangroves</a>. The country had a 2019 <a href="/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index" title="Forest Landscape Integrity Index">Forest Landscape Integrity Index</a> mean score of 5.88/10, ranking it 92nd globally out of 172 countries.<sup id="cite_ref-FLII-Supplementary_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FLII-Supplementary-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At least five parks and reserves have been established: <a href="/wiki/Abdoulaye_Faunal_Reserve" title="Abdoulaye Faunal Reserve">Abdoulaye Faunal Reserve</a>, <a href="/wiki/Fazao_Malfakassa_National_Park" title="Fazao Malfakassa National Park">Fazao Malfakassa National Park</a>, <a href="/wiki/Fosse_aux_Lions_National_Park" title="Fosse aux Lions National Park">Fosse aux Lions National Park</a>, <a href="/wiki/Koutammakou" title="Koutammakou">Koutammakou</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/K%C3%A9ran_National_Park" title="Kéran National Park">Kéran National Park</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Economy">Economy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Togo&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Economy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_Togo" title="Economy of Togo">Economy of Togo</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/List_of_companies_based_in_Togo" class="mw-redirect" title="List of companies based in Togo">List of companies based in Togo</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Togo_Product_Exports_(2019).svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/Togo_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/220px-Togo_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="143" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/Togo_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/330px-Togo_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/Togo_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/440px-Togo_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1100" data-file-height="715" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Togo_Product_Exports_(2019).svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A proportional representation of Togo exports, 2019</div></div></div>
<p>The country possesses <a href="/wiki/Phosphate" title="Phosphate">phosphate</a> deposits<sup id="cite_ref-icye.org_13-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-icye.org-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> and an export sector based on agricultural products such as <a href="/wiki/Coffee" title="Coffee">coffee</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cocoa_bean" title="Cocoa bean">cocoa bean</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Peanut" title="Peanut">peanuts</a> (groundnuts), which together generate roughly 30% of export earnings.<sup id="cite_ref-icye.org_13-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-icye.org-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>  Cotton is a cash crop.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> The fertile land occupies 11.3% of the country, most of which is developed. Some crops are <a href="/wiki/Cassava" title="Cassava">cassava</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jasmine_rice" title="Jasmine rice">jasmine rice</a>, <a href="/wiki/Maize" title="Maize">maize</a> and <a href="/wiki/Millet" title="Millet">millet</a>. Some other sectors are <a href="/wiki/Brewery" title="Brewery">brewery</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Textile" title="Textile">textile</a> industry. "Low market prices" for Togo's "major export commodities" coupled with the "volatile political situation" of the 1990s and 2000s had a "negative effect" on the economy.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It is listed in the <a href="/wiki/Least_developed_country" class="mw-redirect" title="Least developed country">least developed country</a> group. It serves as a regional commercial and trade center. The government's decade-long efforts supported by the <a href="/wiki/World_Bank" title="World Bank">World Bank</a> and the <a href="/wiki/International_Monetary_Fund" title="International Monetary Fund">International Monetary Fund</a> (IMF) to carry out economic reforms, to encourage investments, and to create the balance between income and consumption has stalled. Political unrest,s including private and public sector, strikes throughout 1992 and 1993, jeopardized the reform program, shrank the tax base, and disrupted economic activities in the country.
</p><p>It imports <a href="/wiki/Machinery" class="mw-redirect" title="Machinery">machinery</a>, equipment, <a href="/wiki/Petroleum" title="Petroleum">petroleum</a> products, and food. Its main import partners are France (21.1%), the Netherlands (12.1%), <a href="/wiki/C%C3%B4te_d%27Ivoire" class="mw-redirect" title="Côte d&#39;Ivoire">Côte d'Ivoire</a> (5.9%), Germany (4.6%), Italy (4.4%), South Africa (4.3%) and China (4.1%). The main exports are <a href="/wiki/Cocoa_bean" title="Cocoa bean">cocoa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Coffee" title="Coffee">coffee</a>, re-export of goods, <a href="/wiki/Phosphates" class="mw-redirect" title="Phosphates">phosphates</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cotton" title="Cotton">cotton</a>. "Major export partners" are <a href="/wiki/Burkina_Faso" title="Burkina Faso">Burkina Faso</a> (16.6%), China (15.4%), the Netherlands (13%), <a href="/wiki/Benin" title="Benin">Benin</a> (9.6%) and <a href="/wiki/Mali" title="Mali">Mali</a> (7.4%).
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Togo-pedaleamundo.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/Togo-pedaleamundo.jpg/220px-Togo-pedaleamundo.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/Togo-pedaleamundo.jpg/330px-Togo-pedaleamundo.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/Togo-pedaleamundo.jpg/440px-Togo-pedaleamundo.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1080" data-file-height="810" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Togo-pedaleamundo.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Fishermen</div></div></div>
<p>In terms of structural reforms, it has made progress in the <a href="/wiki/Liberalization" title="Liberalization">liberalization</a> of the economy, namely in the fields of <a href="/wiki/Trade" title="Trade">trade</a> and port activities. The privatization program of the cotton sector, <a href="/wiki/Telecommunications" title="Telecommunications">telecommunications</a> and water supply has stalled.
</p><p>On 12 January 1994, the devaluation of the currency by 50% provided an impetus to renewed structural adjustment; these efforts were facilitated by the end of strife in 1994 and a return to overt political calm. Progress depends on increased openness in government financial operations (to accommodate increased social service outlays) and possible downsizing of the <a href="/wiki/Armed_forces" class="mw-redirect" title="Armed forces">armed forces</a>, on which the regime has depended to stay in place. Lack of aid and depressed cocoa prices generated a 1% fall in GDP in 1998, with growth resuming in 1999. Togo is a member of the <a href="/wiki/Organization_for_the_Harmonization_of_Business_Law_in_Africa" class="mw-redirect" title="Organization for the Harmonization of Business Law in Africa">Organization for the Harmonization of Business Law in Africa</a> (OHADA).<sup id="cite_ref-ohada.com_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ohada.com-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Agriculture" title="Agriculture">Agriculture</a> is the "backbone" of the economy.<sup id="cite_ref-icye.org_13-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-icye.org-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> A shortage of funds for the purchase of <a href="/wiki/Irrigation" title="Irrigation">irrigation</a> equipment and <a href="/wiki/Fertilizers" class="mw-redirect" title="Fertilizers">fertilizers</a> has reduced agricultural output. Agriculture generated 28.2% of <a href="/wiki/GDP" class="mw-redirect" title="GDP">GDP</a> in 2012 and employed 49% of the working population in 2010. The country is essentially <a href="/wiki/Self-sufficient" class="mw-redirect" title="Self-sufficient">self-sufficient</a> in food production. <a href="/wiki/Livestock" title="Livestock">Livestock</a> production is dominated by <a href="/wiki/Cattle_breeding" class="mw-redirect" title="Cattle breeding">cattle breeding</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-cia.gov_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cia.gov-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Mining" title="Mining">Mining</a> generated about 33.9% of GDP in 2012 and employed 12% of the population in 2010. Togo has the fourth-largest <a href="/wiki/Phosphate" title="Phosphate">phosphate</a> deposits in the world. Their production is 2.1 million tons per year. There are reserves of <a href="/wiki/Limestone" title="Limestone">limestone</a>, <a href="/wiki/Marble" title="Marble">marble</a> and <a href="/wiki/Salt" title="Salt">salt</a>. Industry provides 20.4% of Togo's national income, as it consists of light industries and builders. Some reserves of limestone allows Togo to produce <a href="/wiki/Cement" title="Cement">cement</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-cia.gov_58-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cia.gov-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Transport">Transport</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Togo&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Transport">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Transport_in_Togo" title="Transport in Togo">Transport in Togo</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Road">Road</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Togo&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Road">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Togo has a road network of 7,520&#160;km (4,670&#160;mi) as of 2000, with no updated data as of 2023. It has only two major highways, Highway N1 and N2, connecting the capital, Lomé with the city of <a href="/wiki/Dapaong" title="Dapaong">Dapaong</a>, where it gets diverged northwards to <a href="/wiki/Burkina_Faso" title="Burkina Faso">Burkina Faso</a> and from there north-west to <a href="/wiki/Mali" title="Mali">Mali</a>, and north-east to <a href="/wiki/Niger" title="Niger">Niger</a>. N1 is the longest highway of Togo, at a length of 613&#160;km (381&#160;mi). N2 connects Lomé with <a href="/wiki/Aneho" class="mw-redirect" title="Aneho">Aneho</a>. The extension of N2 is Highway RNIE1, or the <a href="/wiki/Trans%E2%80%93West_African_Coastal_Highway" title="Trans–West African Coastal Highway">Trans–West African Coastal Highway</a>, from Aneho to <a href="/wiki/Cotonou" title="Cotonou">Cotonou</a> in <a href="/wiki/Benin" title="Benin">Benin</a>. Other roads and highways are local and regional roads in the rest of the country, also passing through borders with the neighbouring countries. The Trans–West African Coastal Highway crosses Togo, connecting it to <a href="/wiki/Benin" title="Benin">Benin</a> and <a href="/wiki/Nigeria" title="Nigeria">Nigeria</a> to the east, and <a href="/wiki/Ghana" title="Ghana">Ghana</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ivory_Coast" title="Ivory Coast">Ivory Coast</a> to the west. Once the construction in <a href="/wiki/Liberia" title="Liberia">Liberia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sierra_Leone" title="Sierra Leone">Sierra Leone</a> part gets completed, the highway will continue west to seven other <a href="/wiki/Economic_Community_of_West_African_States" title="Economic Community of West African States">Economic Community of West African States</a> (ECOWAS) nations.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Railways">Railways</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Togo&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Railways">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Railways_in_Togo.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Railways_in_Togo.svg/220px-Railways_in_Togo.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="157" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Railways_in_Togo.svg/330px-Railways_in_Togo.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Railways_in_Togo.svg/440px-Railways_in_Togo.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="350" data-file-height="250" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Railways_in_Togo.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Railway network of Togo</div></div></div>
<p>Togo has a railway network of 568&#160;km (353&#160;mi) as of 2008, with no further updates in the network as of 2023. It follows a <a href="/wiki/Track_gauge" title="Track gauge">track gauge</a> of 1,000 mm (3 ft 3+3⁄8 in) (<a href="/wiki/Narrow-gauge_railway" title="Narrow-gauge railway">narrow gauge</a>) Trains are operated by <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">Société Nationale des Chemins de Fer Togolais</i></span> (SNCT), which was established as a result of the restructuring and renaming of <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">Réseau des Chemins de Fer du Togo</i></span> from 1997 to 1998.<sup id="cite_ref-JWR0506_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JWR0506-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Between Hahotoé and the port of Kpémé, the <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">Compagnie Togolaise des Mines du Bénin</i></span> (CTMB) operated phosphate trains.<sup id="cite_ref-JWR0506_61-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JWR0506-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Togo_Eisenbahn_086_1990-02.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/Togo_Eisenbahn_086_1990-02.jpg/220px-Togo_Eisenbahn_086_1990-02.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="144" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/Togo_Eisenbahn_086_1990-02.jpg/330px-Togo_Eisenbahn_086_1990-02.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/Togo_Eisenbahn_086_1990-02.jpg/440px-Togo_Eisenbahn_086_1990-02.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1176" data-file-height="768" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Togo_Eisenbahn_086_1990-02.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A train from <a href="/wiki/Lom%C3%A9" title="Lomé">Lomé</a> to <a href="/wiki/Kpalim%C3%A9" title="Kpalimé">Kpalimé</a>, at an intermediate station</div></div></div>
<p>The following are the railway networks present in the country:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Lom%C3%A9%E2%80%93An%C3%A9ho_railway" title="Lomé–Aného railway">Lomé–Aného railway</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Lom%C3%A9%E2%80%93Blitta_railway" title="Lomé–Blitta railway">Lomé–Blitta railway</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Lom%C3%A9%E2%80%93Kpalim%C3%A9_railway" title="Lomé–Kpalimé railway">Lomé–Kpalimé railway</a></li>
<li>Hahotoé–Kpémé railway (operated by CTMB)<sup id="cite_ref-JWR0506_61-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JWR0506-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Air">Air</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Togo&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Air">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:2014-06-16_17-21-49_Togo_Maritime_-_Station_M%C3%A9t%C3%A9o.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/2014-06-16_17-21-49_Togo_Maritime_-_Station_M%C3%A9t%C3%A9o.JPG/220px-2014-06-16_17-21-49_Togo_Maritime_-_Station_M%C3%A9t%C3%A9o.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="124" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/2014-06-16_17-21-49_Togo_Maritime_-_Station_M%C3%A9t%C3%A9o.JPG/330px-2014-06-16_17-21-49_Togo_Maritime_-_Station_M%C3%A9t%C3%A9o.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/2014-06-16_17-21-49_Togo_Maritime_-_Station_M%C3%A9t%C3%A9o.JPG/440px-2014-06-16_17-21-49_Togo_Maritime_-_Station_M%C3%A9t%C3%A9o.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1986" data-file-height="1117" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:2014-06-16_17-21-49_Togo_Maritime_-_Station_M%C3%A9t%C3%A9o.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Lom%C3%A9%E2%80%93Tokoin_International_Airport" title="Lomé–Tokoin International Airport">Lomé–Tokoin International Airport</a></div></div></div> 
<p>Togo has a total of eight airports, as of 2012, out of which two are <a href="/wiki/International_airport" title="International airport">international airports</a> and six are <a href="/wiki/Domestic_airports" class="mw-redirect" title="Domestic airports">domestic airports</a>. The only major airport of the country is <a href="/wiki/Lom%C3%A9%E2%80%93Tokoin_International_Airport" title="Lomé–Tokoin International Airport">Lomé–Tokoin International Airport</a> serving the capital, Lomé, and another <a href="/wiki/Niamtougou_International_Airport" title="Niamtougou International Airport">Niamtougou International Airport</a> in <a href="/wiki/Niamtougou" title="Niamtougou">Niamtougou</a>, serving the country's northern part.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Water">Water</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Togo&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Water">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:D%C3%A9placement_de_containers_au_port.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/D%C3%A9placement_de_containers_au_port.jpg/220px-D%C3%A9placement_de_containers_au_port.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/D%C3%A9placement_de_containers_au_port.jpg/330px-D%C3%A9placement_de_containers_au_port.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/D%C3%A9placement_de_containers_au_port.jpg/440px-D%C3%A9placement_de_containers_au_port.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3648" data-file-height="2736" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:D%C3%A9placement_de_containers_au_port.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Port of Lomé</div></div></div>
<p>Togo, in terms of water transport, is only 50&#160;km (31&#160;mi) navigable, mostly seasonally on the <a href="/wiki/Mono_River" title="Mono River">Mono River</a>, depending on rainfall, as of 2011. Togo has only one large container port for carrying trade operations in and out of the country, the Port of Lomé, in the capital.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Demographics">Demographics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Togo&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Demographics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Demographics_of_Togo" title="Demographics of Togo">Demographics of Togo</a></div>

<p>The November 2010 census gave Togo a population of 6,191,155, more than double the total counted in the last census, in 2022 the Togo population is 8 680 832.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> That census, taken in 1981, showed the nation had a population of 2,719,567. The capital, Lomé, grew from 375,499 in 1981 to 837,437 in 2010. When the urban population of surrounding Golfe prefecture is added, the Lomé Agglomeration contained 1,477,660 residents in 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Données_de_Recensement_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Données_de_Recensement-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Other cities in Togo according to the new census were Sokodé (95,070), Kara (94,878), Kpalimé (75,084), Atakpamé (69,261), Dapaong (58,071) and Tsévié (54,474). With an estimated population of 8,644,829 (as of 2021<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Togo&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>), Togo is the 107th largest country by population. Most of the population (65%) live in rural villages dedicated to agriculture or pastures. The population of Togo shows a stronger growth: from 1961 (the year after independence) to 2003 it quintupled.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_63-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Données_de_Recensement_64-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Données_de_Recensement-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
